<a id='question-009'></a>

### Question 009: B and T Lymphocyte Activation: Part 1 Focus 09 (antigen)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of antigen presentation by MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) to CD4+ T helper cells, specifically focusing on the requirement for processing and presentation of exogenous antigens for T cell activation. (理解抗原呈递细胞 (APCs) 通过 MHC II 类分子向 CD4+ T 辅助细胞呈递外源性抗原的作用，特别是 T 细胞活化需要处理和呈递外源性抗原。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of well-controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, chills, productive cough, and pleuritic chest pain. He is a retired smoker (20 pack-years) and denies recent travel or sick contacts. Physical examination reveals a temperature of 39.2°C, respiratory rate of 28 breaths/min, and crackles in the right lower lobe. Chest X-ray shows a right lower lobe consolidation. Sputum Gram stain reveals numerous Gram-positive diplococci. Sputum culture grows *Streptococcus pneumoniae*. The patient is treated with intravenous ceftriaxone and azithromycin. Despite initial improvement, he develops acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) characterized by severe hypoxemia and bilateral pulmonary infiltrates on imaging. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid analysis shows a significant increase in neutrophils and inflammatory cytokines, including IL-1β, IL-6, and TNF-α. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) reveals a markedly reduced number of CD4+ T cells capable of proliferating in response to *S. pneumoniae* polysaccharide antigen stimulation. Further investigation reveals that these CD4+ T cells fail to upregulate CD69 expression upon stimulation with *S. pneumoniae* polysaccharide antigen presented by dendritic cells.

Which of the following is the most likely mechanism underlying the impaired CD4+ T cell response in this patient?

(A) Defective internalization of *S. pneumoniae* polysaccharide antigen by dendritic cells.
(B) Impaired processing of *S. pneumoniae* polysaccharide antigen into peptides by dendritic cells.
(C) Failure of MHC class I molecules on dendritic cells to present *S. pneumoniae* peptides to CD8+ T cells.
(D) Defective presentation of *S. pneumoniae* peptides on MHC class II molecules by dendritic cells.
(E) Inability of the CD4+ T cells to recognize the *S. pneumoniae* polysaccharide antigen directly via their T cell receptor (TCR).

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with diabetes, smoking history, presents with symptoms and signs of community-acquired pneumonia (CAP) caused by *Streptococcus pneumoniae*.
- **Microbiology:** Gram-positive diplococci on sputum Gram stain, *S. pneumoniae* confirmed by culture. This establishes the causative agent.
- **Clinical Course:** Initial improvement followed by ARDS, indicating an exaggerated inflammatory response (cytokine storm).
- **Immunological Findings:** Reduced number of CD4+ T cells proliferating in response to *S. pneumoniae* polysaccharide antigen. Failure of these CD4+ T cells to upregulate CD69 (an early activation marker) upon stimulation with *S. pneumoniae* polysaccharide antigen presented by dendritic cells.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism underlying the impaired CD4+ T cell response.

The key is that *S. pneumoniae* is a bacterium, and bacterial antigens (like its polysaccharide capsule) are exogenous. CD4+ T helper cells recognize antigens presented on MHC class II molecules. MHC class II molecules present peptides derived from exogenous antigens that have been internalized, processed (broken down into peptides), and loaded onto the MHC II molecule within the APC (like dendritic cells). The vignette describes a failure of CD4+ T cells to respond to *S. pneumoniae* polysaccharide antigen presented by dendritic cells. The failure to upregulate CD69, an early activation marker, strongly suggests a defect in the initial antigen presentation or recognition step by the T cell. Since *S. pneumoniae* is a bacterium, its antigens are exogenous and should be presented via MHC class II to CD4+ T cells. Therefore, a defect in MHC class II presentation is the most likely explanation for the impaired CD4+ T cell response.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Antigen Presentation:** CD4+ T helper cells recognize peptide antigens presented by MHC class II molecules on APCs (dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells).
- **Exogenous Antigen Pathway:** Extracellular pathogens like *S. pneumoniae* are taken up by APCs via phagocytosis or endocytosis.
- **Processing:** Within endosomes/lysosomes, the ingested pathogen is degraded into peptide fragments by proteases (e.g., cathepsins).
- **Loading:** MHC class II molecules (alpha and beta chains, plus invariant chain initially) are synthesized in the ER, trafficked to endosomes, where they bind the processed peptide fragments. The invariant chain is removed, leaving a peptide-MHC II complex on the cell surface.
- **T Cell Activation:** The peptide-MHC II complex is recognized by the TCR on the CD4+ T cell. Costimulatory signals (e.g., B7 on APC binding CD28 on T cell) and cytokines are also required for full T cell activation.
- **ARDS Pathogenesis:** In severe pneumococcal pneumonia, the host immune response can become dysregulated, leading to excessive cytokine release (cytokine storm) and ARDS. This often involves hyperactive T cell responses, but in this specific case, the vignette points to a *deficient* CD4+ T cell response to the polysaccharide antigen, which might paradoxically contribute to the uncontrolled inflammation by failing to properly regulate the response.
- **Polysaccharide Antigens:** Bacterial polysaccharides (like the *S. pneumoniae* capsule) are T-independent antigens, meaning they can directly activate B cells without T cell help. However, T cell help is crucial for isotype switching, affinity maturation, and memory B cell formation. CD4+ T cells recognize peptides derived from these polysaccharides after processing and presentation on MHC II.

The vignette describes a failure of CD4+ T cells to respond to *S. pneumoniae* polysaccharide antigen presented by dendritic cells. This points directly to a problem with the exogenous antigen processing and presentation pathway involving MHC class II.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Defective internalization of *S. pneumoniae* polysaccharide antigen by dendritic cells. While impaired internalization could lead to reduced antigen presentation, the primary defect described is the failure of T cell activation *after* presentation by dendritic cells. The vignette doesn't provide evidence against internalization itself. Dendritic cells are professional phagocytes and efficiently internalize bacteria.
- (B) Impaired processing of *S. pneumoniae* polysaccharide antigen into peptides by dendritic cells. Polysaccharides are generally poor immunogens and are not efficiently processed into peptides for presentation on MHC class II. While some processing can occur, the primary mechanism for T cell recognition of polysaccharide antigens involves B cell receptors (BCRs) directly recognizing the intact polysaccharide. However, the vignette specifically states the antigen is being presented by dendritic cells to CD4+ T cells, implying a peptide form is involved or expected. A defect in processing *could* be the issue, but option (D) is more directly related to the known pathway for exogenous antigen presentation to CD4+ T cells. Furthermore, the failure to upregulate CD69 suggests the T cell isn't receiving the necessary signal *from* the presented antigen/MHC complex, which is most directly linked to the MHC II presentation itself.
- (C) Failure of MHC class I molecules on dendritic cells to present *S. pneumoniae* peptides to CD8+ T cells. MHC class I molecules present endogenous antigens (e.g., viral proteins synthesized within the cell or peptides from phagocytosed bacteria processed in the cytosol) to CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs). *S. pneumoniae* is an extracellular bacterium, and its antigens are primarily handled via the exogenous pathway (MHC class II) for presentation to CD4+ T cells. While cross-presentation (presenting exogenous antigens on MHC I) can occur, the primary T cell response to bacteria is mediated by CD4+ T cells recognizing MHC II presentation. The vignette focuses on the impaired CD4+ T cell response.
- (E) Inability of the CD4+ T cells to recognize the *S. pneumoniae* polysaccharide antigen directly via their T cell receptor (TCR). TCRs recognize peptide antigens presented by MHC molecules, not intact polysaccharides. While B cells can recognize polysaccharides directly via their BCRs, T cells require processed peptides. This option misunderstands the fundamental mechanism of T cell antigen recognition.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (D) - Defective MHC II Presentation | (A) Defective Internalization | (B) Defective Processing | (C) Defective MHC I Presentation | (E) TCR Recognizes Polysaccharide |
|-------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------|---------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Antigen Type**         | Exogenous (Bacterial)                               | Exogenous (Bacterial)            | Exogenous (Bacterial)    | Endogenous/Cross-presented      | Exogenous (Bacterial)             |
| **Presenting Cell**      | APC (Dendritic Cell)                               | APC (Dendritic Cell)             | APC (Dendritic Cell)      | APC (Dendritic Cell)             | B Cell                             |
| **MHC Molecule**        | MHC Class II                                       | MHC Class II                    | MHC Class II             | MHC Class I                      | N/A (BCR)                          |
| **T Cell Type**         | CD4+ T Helper Cell                                 | CD4+ T Helper Cell               | CD4+ T Helper Cell        | CD8+ T Cytotoxic Cell            | B Cell                             |
| **Mechanism**           | Failure to load/present peptide on MHC II           | Failure to take up antigen      | Failure to cleave peptide | Failure to present peptide on MHC I| Direct BCR recognition             |
| **Relevance to Vignette**| Explains impaired CD4+ T cell response to bacterial antigen | Less likely primary defect given APC function | Possible, but less direct than MHC II defect | Irrelevant to CD4+ T cell response | Irrelevant to T cell response      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CD4+ T cells recognize antigens derived from exogenous pathogens (like bacteria) presented on MHC class II molecules by APCs. Defects in any step of this pathway (internalization, processing, loading onto MHC II, or surface expression of MHC II) can impair CD4+ T cell activation. This vignette highlights the critical role of MHC class II presentation for initiating adaptive immunity against extracellular bacteria. Key differentials include defects in MHC class II expression (e.g., Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome), defects in antigen processing (e.g., TAP deficiency affects MHC I, not II), and defects in T cell signaling or costimulation.

<a id='question-010'></a>

### Question 010: B and T Lymphocyte Activation: Part 1 Focus 10 (processing)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Antigen processing and presentation by MHC class I and II molecules are crucial for initiating adaptive immune responses. Defective antigen processing leads to impaired T cell activation and subsequent immune dysfunction. (抗原加工与提呈是启动适应性免疫的关键。抗原加工缺陷导致T细胞激活受损和随后的免疫功能障碍。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the clinic with a 6-month history of recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia and sinusitis, despite receiving standard vaccinations. She has no significant past medical history and is not immunocompromised due to HIV or medication use. Physical examination reveals normal vital signs and no lymphadenopathy or splenomegaly. Laboratory tests show normal immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE) and normal B cell counts. However, T cell counts are normal, but T cell proliferation in response to mitogens (PHA, ConA) and specific antigens (e.g., Candida albicans) is markedly reduced. Flow cytometry analysis reveals normal expression of CD3, CD4, and CD8 surface markers on T cells. Further investigation reveals a defect in the TAP1 gene, leading to impaired transport of peptides into the endoplasmic reticulum. Which of the following best describes the underlying immunological defect in this patient?

(A) Impaired processing of exogenous antigens for presentation on MHC class II molecules.
(B) Defective expression of MHC class I molecules on the cell surface.
(C) Impaired processing of endogenous antigens for presentation on MHC class I molecules.
(D) Defective expression of CD4 co-stimulatory molecules on antigen-presenting cells.
(E) Impaired production of IL-2 by activated T cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections:** Suggests a defect in cell-mediated immunity (T cells) or antibody production (B cells), as these are crucial for clearing extracellular bacteria and intracellular pathogens common in these sites.
- **Normal immunoglobulin levels and B cell counts:** Rules out primary humoral immunodeficiencies like X-linked agammaglobulinemia or common variable immunodeficiency (CVID).
- **Normal T cell counts:** Rules out conditions like DiGeorge syndrome (thymic hypoplasia) or severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) affecting T cell development.
- **Markedly reduced T cell proliferation:** Indicates a functional defect in T cell activation, despite normal numbers and surface markers.
- **Defect in the TAP1 gene:** This is the key clue. TAP1 (Transporter associated with Antigen Processing 1) forms a complex with TAP2 and tapasin to transport peptides derived from cytosolic proteins into the endoplasmic reticulum (ER).
- **TAP1 defect impairs peptide transport into the ER:** This prevents the loading of MHC class I molecules with processed endogenous antigens (e.g., viral proteins, tumor antigens).
- **Conclusion:** The patient has a defect in the processing and presentation of endogenous antigens via MHC class I, leading to impaired CD8+ T cell activation and function, explaining the recurrent infections.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Antigen Processing and Presentation:** Adaptive immunity relies on the presentation of processed antigens by MHC molecules to T cells.
    - **MHC Class I:** Presents peptides derived from *endogenous* antigens (e.g., viral proteins synthesized within the cell, tumor antigens, self-proteins) to CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs). The pathway involves:
        1.  Cytosolic protein degradation by the proteasome.
        2.  Transport of peptides into the ER via the TAP1/TAP2 transporter complex.
        3.  Loading of peptides onto MHC class I heavy chains in the ER, assisted by chaperones like calnexin, calreticulin, and tapasin.
        4.  Trafficking of the stable MHC class I-peptide complex to the cell surface.
    - **MHC Class II:** Presents peptides derived from *exogenous* antigens (e.g., bacteria phagocytosed by APCs) to CD4+ helper T cells. The pathway involves:
        1.  Phagocytosis/endocytosis of exogenous antigen.
        2.  Degradation of antigen within endosomes/lysosomes by proteases.
        3.  Loading of peptides onto MHC class II molecules within endosomes/lysosomes, facilitated by the invariant chain (Ii).
        4.  Trafficking of the stable MHC class II-peptide complex to the cell surface.
- **TAP1/TAP2 Deficiency (Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome Type I):** Mutations in TAP1 or TAP2 genes lead to a severe deficiency in MHC class I expression on the surface of most nucleated cells. This results in impaired presentation of endogenous antigens to CD8+ T cells. Clinically, it presents with recurrent viral infections (especially herpesviruses like HSV, VZV, EBV) and opportunistic infections, similar to severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID), but B cell function is typically normal. The patient's presentation (recurrent infections, normal B cells/Ig, impaired T cell proliferation) and the specific TAP1 defect strongly point to this diagnosis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Impaired processing of exogenous antigens for presentation on MHC class II molecules. This pathway involves endosomes/lysosomes and proteases like cathepsins, not TAP1. Defects here would affect CD4+ T cell activation and humoral immunity (e.g., MHC class II deficiency, Hermansky-Pudlak syndrome). The patient has normal B cells and Ig, making this less likely.
- (B) Defective expression of MHC class I molecules on the cell surface. While TAP1 deficiency *leads* to defective surface expression of MHC class I, the *underlying defect* is the impaired processing and transport of peptides into the ER, which *prevents* proper MHC class I folding and trafficking. Option (C) describes the primary mechanism more accurately. TAP1 deficiency is a specific cause of impaired MHC class I expression, but the question asks for the *defect* itself, which is the processing step.
- (D) Defective expression of CD4 co-stimulatory molecules on antigen-presenting cells. CD4 is a co-receptor on T cells, not a co-stimulatory molecule on APCs. Co-stimulatory molecules on APCs include B7-1 (CD80) and B7-2 (CD86), which bind to CD28 on T cells. Defects in co-stimulation (e.g., CD80/86 deficiency) lead to anergy or impaired T cell activation, but the patient's defect is specifically related to antigen processing via TAP1.
- (E) Impaired production of IL-2 by activated T cells. IL-2 is crucial for T cell proliferation and function. Defects in IL-2 production or signaling (e.g., IL-2 receptor deficiency) cause severe T cell dysfunction. However, the primary defect in this patient is upstream – the inability to process and present antigens via MHC class I, which prevents proper T cell activation and subsequent IL-2 production. The impaired proliferation is a *consequence* of the processing defect, not the primary defect itself.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                     | TAP1 Deficiency (Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome Type I) | MHC Class II Deficiency | CD80/86 Deficiency (Co-stimulation) | IL-2 Receptor Deficiency |
| :-------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :---------------------- | :--------------------------------- | :----------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**          | TAP1/TAP2 function (peptide transport to ER)     | MHC Class II expression | CD80/CD86 expression on APCs       | IL-2 receptor signaling |
| **Affected T Cell Subset**  | CD8+ T cells primarily                           | CD4+ T cells primarily   | Both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells         | Both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells |
| **MHC Class I Expression**  | Severely reduced/absent                          | Normal                  | Normal                             | Normal                  |
| **MHC Class II Expression** | Normal                                            | Severely reduced/absent | Normal                             | Normal                  |
| **Typical Infections**      | Viral (HSV, VZV, EBV), opportunistic             | Bacterial, fungal       | Variable, often opportunistic      | Severe, broad spectrum  |
| **B Cell Function**         | Normal                                            | Impaired                | Normal                             | Normal                  |
| **Key Mechanism**           | Impaired endogenous antigen processing/presentation | Impaired exogenous antigen processing/presentation | Defective T cell co-stimulation | Defective T cell proliferation signal |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The TAP1/TAP2 complex is essential for transporting peptides from the cytosol into the ER for loading onto MHC class I molecules. Defects in this pathway (e.g., TAP1/TAP2 deficiency) impair the presentation of endogenous antigens to CD8+ T cells, leading to susceptibility to intracellular pathogens, particularly viruses. Differentiate this from defects in MHC class II expression (affecting CD4+ T cells and humoral immunity) or defects in T cell co-stimulation (B7-CD28 pathway).

<a id='question-011'></a>

### Question 011: B and T Lymphocyte Activation: Part 1 Focus 11 (Presentation)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of costimulatory molecules (B7/CD80/CD86 on APCs binding CD28 on T cells) and the T cell receptor (TCR) complex in initiating T cell activation, specifically the requirement for both signals for full activation and the consequences of their absence or dysregulation. (理解共刺激分子 (APC 上的 B7/CD80/CD86 与 T 细胞上的 CD28 结合) 和 T 细胞受体 (TCR) 复合物在启动 T 细胞激活中的作用，特别是对这两个信号的必要性以及它们缺失或失调的后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy with a history of recurrent severe bacterial infections (e.g., *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Streptococcus pneumoniae*) since infancy is brought to the emergency department with fever, chills, and a productive cough. Chest X-ray reveals bilateral pneumonia. Laboratory tests show elevated white blood cell count with neutrophilia. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals a normal number of CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, but these T cells show markedly reduced expression of the CD28 costimulatory molecule. The patient's B cell numbers and immunoglobulin levels are normal. His mother reports that he has never received any vaccinations due to concerns about his frequent infections. Given the patient's clinical presentation and immunological findings, which of the following mechanisms best explains his susceptibility to severe bacterial infections?

(A) Defective antigen presentation due to impaired MHC class II expression on antigen-presenting cells (APCs).
(B) Impaired T cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the CD3 complex.
(C) Failure of T cell activation due to the absence of the necessary costimulatory signal (Signal 2).
(D) Defective B cell maturation leading to impaired antibody production against encapsulated bacteria.
(E) Impaired complement activation leading to reduced opsonization and lysis of bacteria.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 6-year-old boy with recurrent severe bacterial infections since infancy. This points towards an underlying immunodeficiency, likely affecting cell-mediated immunity or antibody production against bacteria.
- **Infections:** *Staphylococcus aureus* and *Streptococcus pneumoniae* are common encapsulated bacteria, suggesting a defect in opsonization or T cell-dependent B cell activation.
- **Current Presentation:** Fever, chills, productive cough, bilateral pneumonia. Consistent with a severe bacterial infection.
- **Lab Findings:** Elevated WBC with neutrophilia (typical acute bacterial infection response). Normal B cell numbers and immunoglobulin levels rule out primary B cell defects like X-linked agammaglobulinemia or common variable immunodeficiency.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Normal CD4+ and CD8+ T cell numbers, but *markedly reduced CD28 expression* on T cells. This is the crucial finding. CD28 is a costimulatory molecule on T cells.
- **Vaccination History:** Never vaccinated due to frequent infections, further supporting a significant immune defect.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining susceptibility to severe bacterial infections.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T Cell Activation:** Naive T cell activation requires two signals:
    - **Signal 1:** TCR engagement with peptide-MHC complex on APCs. This provides antigen specificity.
    - **Signal 2:** Costimulatory signal, primarily CD28 on the T cell binding to B7 molecules (CD80/CD86) on the APC. This confirms the APC is presenting a genuine threat and prevents anergy or apoptosis.
- **CD28/B7 Pathway:** B7 molecules (CD80/CD86) are upregulated on APCs (like dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells) upon activation by PAMPs (via TLRs) or danger signals. CD28 on T cells binds B7, leading to downstream signaling (e.g., PI3K activation) crucial for T cell proliferation, cytokine production (like IL-2), and survival.
- **Consequences of CD28 Deficiency:** Without CD28 (or B7), T cells receive only Signal 1. This can lead to:
    - **Anergy:** T cells become unresponsive to antigen.
    - **Apoptosis:** T cells undergo programmed cell death.
    - **Impaired T cell function:** Reduced proliferation, cytokine production (especially IL-2), and effector function.
- **Clinical Relevance:** CD28 deficiency (e.g., in CTLA-4 deficiency, which paradoxically leads to autoimmunity due to unopposed CD28 signaling, or in rare genetic CD28 deficiency) results in severe immunodeficiency, particularly susceptibility to intracellular pathogens and encapsulated bacteria (due to impaired T cell help for B cells). The patient's presentation fits this pattern.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** The patient has normal B cells and Ig levels, ruling out primary antibody deficiencies. The normal T cell numbers but defective CD28 expression point specifically to a defect in T cell costimulation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Defective antigen presentation due to impaired MHC class II expression on antigen-presenting cells (APCs). This would cause Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome (BLS), leading to a lack of CD4+ T cells because they cannot receive Signal 1. The vignette states normal CD4+ T cell numbers, making this incorrect. BLS typically presents earlier and more severely, often with failure to thrive.
- (B) Impaired T cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the CD3 complex. Mutations in the CD3 complex (part of the TCR signaling machinery) cause severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or T cell activation defects. While this impairs Signal 1, the key finding here is the *lack of CD28* (Signal 2), not a primary defect in the TCR complex itself. SCID usually presents earlier and involves both T and B cell defects.
- (D) Defective B cell maturation leading to impaired antibody production against encapsulated bacteria. This describes conditions like X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA) or common variable immunodeficiency (CVID). However, the vignette explicitly states that the patient has normal B cell numbers and immunoglobulin levels, ruling out these primary B cell defects.
- (E) Impaired complement activation leading to reduced opsonization and lysis of bacteria. Complement deficiencies (e.g., C3 deficiency) can increase susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria. However, this would not explain the specific finding of reduced CD28 expression on T cells. Complement deficiencies typically present with recurrent infections, but the specific T cell defect points away from this mechanism as the primary cause.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | CD28 Deficiency (Correct Answer) | MHC Class II Deficiency (BLS) | CD3 Deficiency (SCID/T-cell defect) | B Cell Deficiency (XLA/CVID) | Complement Deficiency |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :--------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | CD28 (Signal 2)                   | MHC Class II (Signal 1)        | CD3 (Signal 1)                      | B Cell Development/Function | Complement Components |
| **T Cell Numbers**      | Normal                            | Low (CD4+)                     | Low (CD4+ & CD8+)                   | Normal                     | Normal                |
| **B Cell Numbers**      | Normal                            | Normal                        | Normal                              | Low (XLA) / Normal (CVID)   | Normal                |
| **Ig Levels**           | Normal                            | Normal                        | Low                                 | Low                         | Normal                |
| **CD28 Expression**     | Low/Absent                        | Normal                        | Normal                              | Normal                     | Normal                |
| **Key Infections**      | Encapsulated bacteria, intracellular | Severe, broad (opportunistic) | Severe, broad (opportunistic)       | Encapsulated bacteria      | Encapsulated bacteria  |
| **Mechanism**           | Impaired T cell activation (Signal 2) | Impaired T cell activation (Signal 1) | Impaired T cell activation (Signal 1) | Impaired antibody production | Impaired opsonization/lysis |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The vignette highlights the critical role of costimulation (Signal 2 via CD28-B7 interaction) in T cell activation, alongside antigen recognition (Signal 1 via TCR-MHC). Absence of costimulation leads to T cell anergy or apoptosis and immunodeficiency. Key differentials include defects in MHC expression (BLS), TCR signaling (SCID), B cell function (XLA/CVID), and complement (increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria, but no specific T cell defect).

<a id='question-012'></a>

### Question 012: B and T Lymphocyte Activation: Part 1 Focus 12 (Explain)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of costimulatory signals (specifically CD28-B7 interaction) in T cell activation, and how their absence or blockade leads to T cell anergy or tolerance, preventing autoimmune responses. (理解共刺激信号（特别是CD28-B7相互作用）在T细胞活化中的作用，以及其缺乏或阻断如何导致T细胞无能或耐受性，从而防止自身免疫反应。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, chills, nausea, vomiting, and diffuse abdominal pain. She is a known type 1 diabetic and has been meticulously managing her condition with insulin injections for the past 15 years. She reports no recent travel, sick contacts, or new medications, except for her regular insulin regimen. Physical examination reveals diffuse abdominal tenderness, guarding, and rebound tenderness, suggestive of peritonitis. Laboratory studies show leukocytosis with a left shift, elevated C-reactive protein (CRP), and elevated blood glucose. An exploratory laparotomy reveals a perforated appendix with widespread purulent peritonitis. Cultures of the peritoneal fluid grow *Escherichia coli*. Postoperatively, the patient develops severe sepsis and multi-organ failure. Despite aggressive fluid resuscitation and broad-spectrum antibiotics, her condition deteriorates rapidly. Her physician decides to administer an experimental therapy targeting T cell activation to prevent a potential cytokine storm. The therapy involves a monoclonal antibody that specifically blocks the interaction between CD28 on T cells and B7 ligands (CD80/CD86) on antigen-presenting cells (APCs). Following administration of this antibody, the patient's inflammatory markers (CRP, IL-6) decrease significantly, and her clinical condition stabilizes.

Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains the therapeutic effect of the monoclonal antibody in this patient?

(A) Inhibition of T cell receptor (TCR) signaling by preventing phosphorylation of intracellular tyrosine kinases.
(B) Blockade of the interaction between CD40L on T cells and CD40 on B cells, preventing isotype switching and antibody production.
(C) Prevention of the interaction between CD28 on T cells and B7 ligands on APCs, thereby inhibiting the delivery of the second signal required for full T cell activation.
(D) Interference with the binding of MHC class II molecules on APCs to the TCR on CD4+ T cells, preventing antigen presentation.
(E) Inhibition of the production of interleukin-2 (IL-2) by blocking the interaction between the IL-2 receptor and its ligand.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** A 32-year-old female with type 1 diabetes develops perforated appendicitis leading to severe sepsis and multi-organ failure. This suggests a massive inflammatory response triggered by the bacterial infection (*E. coli*).
- **Experimental Therapy:** A monoclonal antibody is administered that blocks the interaction between CD28 (on T cells) and B7 ligands (CD80/CD86, on APCs).
- **Outcome:** The patient's inflammatory markers decrease, and her condition stabilizes. This indicates that blocking the CD28-B7 interaction dampened the excessive inflammatory response.
- **Question:** The question asks for the mechanism by which this antibody exerts its therapeutic effect.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T Cell Activation:** Naive T cells require two signals for full activation:
    1.  **Signal 1:** The T cell receptor (TCR) binds to the peptide-MHC complex on the surface of an antigen-presenting cell (APC). This signal initiates TCR signaling but is not sufficient for full activation.
    2.  **Signal 2 (Costimulation):** Costimulatory molecules on the APC bind to their receptors on the T cell. The primary costimulatory pathway involves the interaction between B7 ligands (CD80/CD86) on the APC and CD28 on the T cell. This signal provides the necessary "go" signal for T cell activation, proliferation, cytokine production (like IL-2), and differentiation.
- **Role of CD28-B7:** The CD28-B7 interaction is crucial for preventing T cell anergy (unresponsiveness). If a T cell receives Signal 1 (TCR engagement) but not Signal 2 (costimulation), it may become anergic or undergo apoptosis. In the context of sepsis, massive activation of APCs (like macrophages and dendritic cells) by bacterial products (e.g., LPS from *E. coli*) leads to upregulation of B7 ligands. This, combined with TCR engagement by bacterial antigens, results in widespread T cell activation and a cytokine storm (excessive release of pro-inflammatory cytokines like IL-1, IL-6, TNF-α, IFN-γ), contributing significantly to the pathology of sepsis.
- **Mechanism of the Antibody:** The monoclonal antibody blocks the CD28-B7 interaction. By preventing this interaction, the antibody inhibits the delivery of the second costimulatory signal required for full T cell activation. This leads to reduced T cell proliferation, cytokine production, and overall inflammatory response, thereby mitigating the cytokine storm and improving the patient's condition.
- **Distractor Analysis:**
    - (A) TCR signaling involves phosphorylation of intracellular tyrosine kinases (like Lck, ZAP-70) downstream of the TCR complex. While crucial for Signal 1, blocking the CD28-B7 interaction affects Signal 2, not the initial TCR signaling cascade itself.
    - (B) The CD40L-CD40 interaction is important for T cell-dependent B cell activation, isotype switching, and affinity maturation. While T cells are involved in the inflammatory response, blocking this specific interaction primarily affects B cell function, not the initial T cell activation event targeted by the described antibody.
    - (D) MHC-TCR interaction is Signal 1. Blocking this interaction would prevent antigen recognition and T cell activation altogether, which is not the specific mechanism of the CD28-B7 blocking antibody. The antibody targets the costimulatory signal (Signal 2).
    - (E) IL-2 is a key T cell growth factor produced *after* T cell activation (partially dependent on CD28 costimulation). Blocking the IL-2 receptor or its ligand would inhibit T cell proliferation, but the primary mechanism of the antibody described is preventing the initial full activation event that leads to IL-2 production.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): This option describes the mechanism of Signal 1 (TCR engagement), not the effect of blocking the CD28-B7 costimulatory pathway (Signal 2). The antibody targets the interaction between CD28 and B7, which is distinct from the TCR complex and its associated kinases.
- (B): This option describes a crucial interaction for T-dependent B cell responses (CD40L on T cells binding CD40 on B cells). While T cells contribute to inflammation, blocking this pathway primarily affects B cell function (isotype switching, memory B cell formation) rather than the initial T cell activation event targeted by the antibody.
- (D): This option describes Signal 1 (TCR engagement with MHC-peptide). Blocking this interaction would prevent antigen recognition, but the antibody specifically targets the CD28-B7 costimulatory interaction (Signal 2), which occurs *after* TCR engagement.
- (E): This option describes the IL-2 pathway, which is downstream of T cell activation and costimulation. While blocking IL-2 signaling would inhibit T cell proliferation, the antibody acts upstream by preventing the initial full activation signal provided by CD28-B7 interaction, which is necessary for robust IL-2 production in the first place.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Correct Answer (C) - CD28-B7 Blockade | Option (A) - TCR Signaling Blockade | Option (B) - CD40L-CD40 Blockade | Option (D) - MHC-TCR Blockade | Option (E) - IL-2 Blockade |
|----------------------|---------------------------------------|------------------------------------|---------------------------------|-------------------------------|----------------------------|
| **Target Interaction** | CD28 (T cell) - B7 (APC)              | TCR (T cell) - MHC-peptide (APC)   | CD40L (T cell) - CD40 (B cell)  | TCR (T cell) - MHC-peptide (APC)| IL-2 Receptor - IL-2 Ligand |
| **Signal Type**       | Costimulatory (Signal 2)              | Antigen Recognition (Signal 1)    | T-B Cell Interaction           | Antigen Recognition (Signal 1)| Downstream Effector        |
| **Primary Effect**    | Prevents full T cell activation       | Prevents antigen recognition      | Impairs B cell activation      | Prevents antigen recognition | Inhibits T cell proliferation|
| **Relevance to Case** | Dampens cytokine storm in sepsis      | Would prevent initial response   | Less direct effect on sepsis   | Would prevent initial response| Downstream effect          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The CD28-B7 interaction provides the essential second signal (costimulation) for naive T cell activation. Blocking this interaction prevents full T cell activation and cytokine production, which can be therapeutically useful in conditions involving excessive T cell responses, such as sepsis or autoimmune diseases. Key differentials include understanding the roles of Signal 1 (TCR-MHC) and other costimulatory pathways (e.g., CD40L-CD40, ICOS-ICOSL).

<a id='question-013'></a>

### Question 013: B and T Lymphocyte Activation: Part 1 Focus 13 (activation)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of costimulatory signals (specifically CD28-B7 interaction) in T cell activation, and how the absence of these signals leads to anergy or tolerance, particularly in the context of antigen presentation by non-professional APCs or in the absence of danger signals. (理解共刺激信号（特别是CD28-B7相互作用）在T细胞活化中的作用，以及缺乏这些信号如何导致T细胞无能或耐受，特别是在非专业抗原提呈细胞呈抗原或缺乏危险信号的情况下。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, chills, and severe headache. He is a research scientist working in a university laboratory. Two weeks prior, he accidentally pricked his finger with a needle contaminated with a novel, genetically engineered protein synthesized in *E. coli*. He has no known allergies or underlying medical conditions. Physical examination reveals a temperature of 39.5°C (103.1°F), tachycardia, and nuchal rigidity. Initial laboratory tests show leukocytosis with neutrophilia. A lumbar puncture is performed, and cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analysis reveals elevated protein, decreased glucose, and numerous Gram-negative rods. Gram stain of the CSF shows pleomorphic, Gram-negative rods. CSF culture grows the organism on MacConkey agar, which is lactose-fermenting and produces H₂S. Serological testing reveals high titers of IgM antibodies against the novel protein, but no significant IgG response. Flow cytometry analysis of his peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a decreased percentage of CD4+ T cells expressing CD25 and CD69, and a decreased proliferation response to the novel protein antigen presented by dendritic cells.

Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains the patient's suboptimal T cell response to the novel protein antigen?

(A) The novel protein antigen is a T-independent antigen that primarily activates B cells via cross-linking of B cell receptors, bypassing the need for T cell help.
(B) The *E. coli*-derived lipopolysaccharide (LPS) acts as a superantigen, causing non-specific T cell activation and cytokine storm, leading to T cell exhaustion.
(C) The novel protein antigen is presented by non-professional antigen-presenting cells (APCs) lacking sufficient B7 costimulatory molecules (CD80/CD86), leading to T cell anergy.
(D) The patient's immune system has developed immunological tolerance to the novel protein antigen due to prolonged exposure in the laboratory setting.
(E) The novel protein antigen is a hapten that requires conjugation to a carrier protein to elicit a T cell-dependent immune response, which has not yet occurred.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, research scientist (potential exposure).
*   **Exposure:** Accidental needle prick with genetically engineered protein synthesized in *E. coli*.
*   **Symptoms:** Fever, chills, headache, nuchal rigidity (suggestive of meningitis).
*   **CSF Findings:** Elevated protein, decreased glucose, Gram-negative rods (bacterial meningitis).
*   **Microbiology:** Gram-negative rods, lactose-fermenting, H₂S production on MacConkey agar (consistent with *E. coli* or related Enterobacteriaceae).
*   **Immunology:** High IgM, low IgG (suggests early or suboptimal response), decreased CD25/CD69 expression and proliferation on T cells in response to the novel protein.
*   **Question:** Mechanism explaining suboptimal T cell response.

The key clues point towards a bacterial infection (*E. coli* likely, given the source and CSF findings) and a suboptimal T cell response to the novel protein antigen, despite IgM production. The decreased CD25/CD69 expression and proliferation indicate a failure of full T cell activation.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **T Cell Activation:** Naive T cells require two signals for full activation:
    1.  Signal 1: T cell receptor (TCR) binding to peptide-MHC complex on an APC.
    2.  Signal 2: Costimulatory signal, primarily CD28 on the T cell binding to B7 molecules (CD80/CD86) on the APC.
*   **Professional vs. Non-professional APCs:** Dendritic cells (DCs) are the most potent professional APCs, expressing high levels of MHC and B7 molecules. Macrophages and B cells are also professional APCs. Non-professional APCs (e.g., fibroblasts, epithelial cells, erythrocytes) express MHC but typically low or no B7 molecules unless activated by inflammatory signals (e.g., PAMPs like LPS via TLRs, or cytokines like IFN-γ).
*   **Consequences of Lack of Costimulation:** If a T cell receives Signal 1 but not Signal 2 (due to presentation by a non-professional APC or lack of danger signals), it can lead to:
    *   **Anergy:** The T cell becomes unresponsive to subsequent stimulation, even if costimulation is provided later.
    *   **Deletion:** The T cell may undergo apoptosis.
    *   **Differentiation into regulatory T cells (Tregs):** In some contexts.
*   **In this case:** The patient was exposed to a novel protein synthesized in *E. coli*. While *E. coli* itself can activate DCs via LPS (a PAMP), the protein itself might be presented by cells that lack sufficient B7 costimulation, especially if the presentation occurs in the absence of strong inflammatory signals. The suboptimal T cell response (decreased activation markers, proliferation) is consistent with a lack of costimulation leading to anergy or tolerance. The IgM response suggests some B cell activation (potentially T-independent or with weak T cell help), but the T cell arm is clearly impaired.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** T-independent antigens (like polysaccharides or LPS) primarily activate B cells without T cell help, leading to IgM production but poor class switching or memory. While the protein might induce some T-independent B cell response, the question focuses on the *suboptimal T cell response*. The scenario describes a novel protein, which typically requires T cell help for a robust response, especially for IgG production and memory. The T cell activation defect is the central issue.
- (B): **Incorrect.** Superantigens cause massive, non-specific T cell activation by cross-linking TCRs and MHC class II molecules outside the normal peptide-binding groove, leading to a cytokine storm and potentially T cell exhaustion or apoptosis. This is characterized by *excessive* T cell activation, not the *suboptimal* activation described (decreased CD25/CD69, proliferation).
- (D): **Incorrect.** Immunological tolerance typically develops through central tolerance (thymic deletion/editing) or peripheral tolerance mechanisms (anergy, suppression by Tregs). While anergy is a form of peripheral tolerance, this option is too general. The specific mechanism described in the vignette (presentation by cells lacking B7) points directly to a failure of costimulation, which is a specific cause of anergy/tolerance, making option (C) more precise. Furthermore, tolerance usually implies a *lack* of response, whereas here there is *some* response (IgM), albeit suboptimal T cell help.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Haptens are small molecules that are not immunogenic on their own and require conjugation to a carrier protein to elicit an immune response. While this can occur, the vignette describes a novel *protein* synthesized in *E. coli*. Proteins are generally immunogenic and do not require conjugation to act as antigens. The issue is not the nature of the antigen (hapten vs. protein) but the *quality* of the T cell response to the presented antigen.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (C) - Lack of B7 Costimulation | Option (A) - T-independent Antigen | Option (B) - Superantigen | Option (D) - Tolerance | Option (E) - Hapten |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :---------------------- | :-------------------- | :----------------- |
| **T Cell Activation**   | Suboptimal (Anergy)                            | Bypassed (No T cell help needed)  | Excessive (Non-specific) | Absent/Suppressed    | Requires Carrier   |
| **APC Type**            | Non-professional (low B7)                      | N/A (B cell activation)            | N/A                    | N/A                  | N/A                |
| **Signal 1**            | Present (TCR-MHC)                              | N/A                               | Present (TCR-MHC)       | Present (TCR-MHC)    | Present (TCR-MHC)  |
| **Signal 2 (Costimulation)** | Absent (No B7)                                | N/A                               | Present (Cross-linking) | Absent/Altered       | N/A                |
| **Outcome**             | Anergy, weak response                          | IgM only, no memory               | Cytokine storm, shock   | Lack of response     | Requires conjugation |
| **Clinical Scenario Fit** | Fits suboptimal T cell response to protein     | Doesn't explain T cell defect     | Opposite effect         | Too general           | Antigen type issue |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is that T cell activation requires both antigen-specific recognition (Signal 1) and costimulation (Signal 2, CD28-B7). Presentation by non-professional APCs lacking B7 molecules leads to T cell anergy or tolerance. Differentiate this from T-independent antigens (bypass T cells), superantigens (cause excessive activation), general tolerance (less specific mechanism), and haptens (require carrier proteins).

<a id='question-014'></a>

### Question 014: B and T Lymphocyte Activation: Part 1 Focus 14 (lymphocytes)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of costimulatory signals (CD28-B7 interaction) in T cell activation and the consequences of their absence or blockade, specifically in the context of anergy or tolerance. (理解T细胞激活中CD28-B7共刺激信号的作用，以及其缺失或阻断导致的细胞无能或耐受。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of well-controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, chills, productive cough, and shortness of breath. He is found to be hypoxic and tachycardic. Chest X-ray reveals bilateral infiltrates consistent with pneumonia. Sputum Gram stain shows numerous Gram-positive cocci in pairs (diplococci). Blood cultures are positive for *Streptococcus pneumoniae*. The patient is started on intravenous ceftriaxone and azithromycin. Despite appropriate antibiotic therapy, his respiratory status deteriorates over the next 48 hours, requiring mechanical ventilation. His white blood cell count remains elevated, but his lymphocyte count is significantly decreased. Flow cytometry analysis of his peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals normal expression of T cell receptor (TCR) and CD3, but markedly reduced expression of CD28 on CD4+ and CD8+ T cells. Further investigation reveals that his T cells, when stimulated *in vitro* with anti-CD3 antibodies and *S. pneumoniae* antigens, fail to proliferate or produce cytokines (IL-2, IFN-γ) despite normal MHC class II expression on antigen-presenting cells.

Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains the patient's impaired T cell response to *Streptococcus pneumoniae* infection?

(A) Defective intracellular signaling downstream of the T cell receptor (TCR) complex due to a mutation in the *ZAP70* gene.
(B) Impaired antigen presentation by dendritic cells due to deficient expression of MHC class II molecules.
(C) Lack of costimulatory signaling due to reduced CD28 expression on T cells, leading to T cell anergy.
(D) Excessive production of immunosuppressive cytokines, such as IL-10 and TGF-β, by regulatory T cells (Tregs).
(E) Inability of B cells to present processed *S. pneumoniae* antigens via MHC class II molecules to CD4+ T cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with pneumonia caused by *Streptococcus pneumoniae*. This establishes the infectious trigger.
- **Clinical Course:** Despite appropriate antibiotics, the patient's condition worsens, suggesting an underlying immune defect impairing pathogen clearance.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Decreased lymphocyte count: Suggests lymphopenia, potentially related to the infection or an underlying immune issue.
    - Flow cytometry: Normal CD3/TCR expression but *markedly reduced CD28* expression on T cells. This is a critical clue pointing towards a defect in costimulation.
    - *In vitro* T cell stimulation: T cells fail to proliferate or produce cytokines (IL-2, IFN-γ) when stimulated with anti-CD3 (TCR engagement) and *S. pneumoniae* antigens (antigen presentation via MHC). Normal MHC class II expression on APCs rules out a primary APC defect.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining the impaired T cell response.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T Cell Activation:** Naive T cell activation requires two signals:
    1.  **Signal 1:** TCR engagement with peptide-MHC complex on an antigen-presenting cell (APC). This signal initiates TCR signaling cascade.
    2.  **Signal 2 (Costimulation):** Interaction between costimulatory molecules on the T cell (CD28) and the APC (B7 molecules: CD80/CD86). This signal is crucial for full T cell activation, proliferation, cytokine production, and survival.
- **Consequences of Absent Costimulation:** If Signal 1 is delivered without Signal 2 (e.g., due to lack of CD28 on T cells or lack of B7 on APCs), the T cell becomes anergic (unresponsive) or undergoes apoptosis. This is a mechanism of peripheral tolerance to prevent autoimmunity.
- **Vignette Analysis:** The patient has *S. pneumoniae* pneumonia. His T cells express TCR/CD3 but lack CD28. When stimulated *in vitro* with anti-CD3 (Signal 1) and antigen (presented by APCs with normal MHC II), the T cells fail to respond (no proliferation, no cytokines). This perfectly matches the scenario of T cell anergy due to the absence of costimulatory Signal 2 (CD28-B7 interaction). The lack of CD28 prevents the necessary second signal for full T cell activation, leading to impaired immunity against the pneumococcal infection.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Defective intracellular signaling downstream of the TCR complex (e.g., *ZAP70* mutation) would impair Signal 1 processing. While this leads to immunodeficiency, the vignette specifically highlights the *lack of CD28* expression and the failure to respond *despite* TCR engagement (anti-CD3 stimulation). *ZAP70* deficiency typically presents earlier in life with severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID)-like symptoms, not as a late-onset susceptibility to bacterial pneumonia with preserved TCR expression.
- (B): Impaired antigen presentation by dendritic cells due to deficient MHC class II expression would prevent Signal 1 delivery. However, the vignette explicitly states that APCs have *normal MHC class II expression*. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (D): Excessive production of immunosuppressive cytokines (IL-10, TGF-β) by Tregs can suppress T cell responses. While Tregs play a role in tolerance, the primary defect identified in this patient is the *lack of CD28* on T cells, which directly explains the failure of activation upon stimulation. There's no specific evidence pointing towards Treg overactivity as the primary cause.
- (E): Inability of B cells to present processed antigens via MHC class II to CD4+ T cells would impair CD4+ T cell help for B cell antibody production and potentially affect CD4+ T cell activation itself. However, the vignette focuses on the *T cell's intrinsic inability to respond* to stimulation (Signal 1 + antigen presentation by APCs), which is directly linked to the lack of CD28. Furthermore, professional APCs like dendritic cells and macrophages are the primary presenters to naive T cells, and their MHC II expression is normal in this case.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (C) - Lack of CD28/Costimulation | (A) Defective TCR Signaling (e.g., ZAP70) | (B) Defective MHC II on APCs | (D) Treg Suppression | (E) Defective B cell MHC II |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :------------------------- | :------------------- | :------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | CD28 deficiency on T cells                      | Intracellular TCR signaling pathway       | MHC II expression on APCs  | Treg activity        | MHC II expression on B cells |
| **TCR Expression**      | Normal                                         | Normal                                    | Normal                    | Normal              | Normal                    |
| **CD28 Expression**     | Reduced/Absent                                 | Normal                                    | N/A                       | Normal              | N/A                       |
| **MHC II on APCs**      | Normal                                         | Normal                                    | Deficient                 | Normal              | Normal                    |
| **Response to Anti-CD3** | No proliferation/cytokines (Anergy)            | No proliferation/cytokines (Defective Sig1) | No proliferation/cytokines | Suppressed response | Impaired CD4+ T cell help |
| **Clinical Manifestation**| Susceptibility to infections (e.g., *S. pneumoniae*) | Severe immunodeficiency (SCID-like)      | Immunodeficiency          | Autoimmunity/Tolerance| Impaired humoral immunity |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
T cell activation requires two signals: TCR engagement (Signal 1) and costimulation (Signal 2, CD28-B7). Absence of costimulation leads to T cell anergy. This is a key mechanism of peripheral tolerance but can also cause immunodeficiency if it affects effector T cells during infection. Differentiate this from defects in TCR signaling (Signal 1), antigen presentation (MHC deficiency), Treg suppression, or B cell function.

<a id='question-015'></a>

### Question 015: B and T Lymphocyte Activation: Part 1 Focus 15 (ACTIVATION)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the sequential steps of T cell activation, including the role of the T cell receptor (TCR) complex, costimulatory molecules (CD28-B7), and cytokine signaling (e.g., IL-2), and how deficiencies in these pathways impact immune responses. (理解T细胞激活的顺序步骤，包括T细胞受体复合物、共刺激分子（CD28-B7）和细胞因子信号（例如IL-2）的作用，以及这些通路缺陷如何影响免疫应答。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, chills, productive cough, and pleuritic chest pain. He is immunocompetent and has no significant past medical history or travel history. Physical examination reveals a temperature of 39.5°C, respiratory rate of 28 breaths/min, and crackles in the right lower lobe. A chest X-ray shows a right lower lobe consolidation. Sputum Gram stain reveals numerous Gram-positive cocci in pairs (diplococci). Sputum culture grows *Streptococcus pneumoniae*. The patient is treated with intravenous ceftriaxone and recovers fully. One week later, a blood sample is analyzed for T cell activation markers following stimulation with anti-CD3 and anti-CD28 antibodies in vitro. Flow cytometry reveals that the patient's T cells exhibit significantly reduced expression of CD25 (IL-2 receptor alpha chain) and decreased proliferation in response to stimulation, despite normal expression of the TCR complex and CD3 signaling molecules.

Which of the following molecules is most likely deficient in this patient's T cells, explaining the impaired activation observed in vitro?

(A) CD3ζ chain
(B) Protein Kinase C (PKC)
(C) CD28
(D) Interleukin-2 (IL-2)
(E) ZAP-70

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 35-year-old immunocompetent male with community-acquired pneumonia (CAP) caused by *Streptococcus pneumoniae*. This establishes the context of an immune response to a common bacterial pathogen.
- **Clinical Course:** Patient recovers fully with antibiotic treatment, suggesting the immune system eventually controlled the infection, although the specific defect is being tested.
- **In Vitro T Cell Activation Assay:** T cells are stimulated with anti-CD3 (mimics TCR engagement) and anti-CD28 (mimics costimulation).
- **Flow Cytometry Results:** Reduced expression of CD25 (IL-2 receptor alpha chain) and decreased proliferation in response to stimulation. Normal TCR complex and CD3 signaling molecules.
- **Key Clue:** The defect occurs *despite* normal TCR engagement (anti-CD3 stimulation) and normal CD3 signaling components (CD3ζ, ZAP-70, PKC). The problem lies in the downstream consequences of TCR engagement, specifically the costimulatory signal and subsequent IL-2 production/response. Reduced CD25 expression indicates impaired IL-2 signaling, and decreased proliferation is a consequence of insufficient IL-2-mediated clonal expansion.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T Cell Activation:** T cell activation requires two signals:
    1.  **Signal 1:** TCR engagement by peptide-MHC complex on an antigen-presenting cell (APC). This involves the TCR complex (TCRαβ, CD3γδεζ) and associated signaling molecules like ZAP-70 and Protein Kinase C (PKC).
    2.  **Signal 2 (Costimulation):** Interaction between CD28 on the T cell and B7 molecules (CD80/CD86) on the APC.
- **Downstream Events:** Signal 1 leads to activation of intracellular signaling cascades (e.g., involving Lck, ZAP-70, PLCγ, PKC, calcineurin). Signal 2 amplifies Signal 1 and is crucial for full T cell activation, survival, and cytokine production.
- **IL-2 Production and Response:** Activated T cells (especially CD4+ T helper cells) produce IL-2, a key cytokine for T cell proliferation and differentiation. IL-2 binds to the IL-2 receptor (IL-2R), which consists of IL-2Rβγ (high affinity) and IL-2Rα (CD25, expressed upon activation, forming a very high affinity receptor). CD25 expression is a marker of T cell activation. Autocrine and paracrine IL-2 signaling via the IL-2R drives T cell clonal expansion (proliferation).
- **Analysis of the Case:** The patient's T cells respond to anti-CD3 (Signal 1 is intact) and have normal CD3 signaling components (CD3ζ, ZAP-70, PKC are functional). However, they show reduced CD25 expression and decreased proliferation. This points to a defect in Signal 2 (costimulation) or the subsequent IL-2 production/response pathway.
- **CD28 Function:** CD28 is the primary costimulatory receptor on T cells. It binds to B7 (CD80/CD86) on APCs. Without CD28-B7 interaction, T cell activation is severely impaired, leading to reduced IL-2 production, reduced CD25 expression, and decreased proliferation, even if Signal 1 is present.
- **Evaluating the Options:**
    - (A) CD3ζ chain: Part of the TCR complex (Signal 1). Deficiency would impair TCR signaling itself, which is stated as normal in the vignette.
    - (B) Protein Kinase C (PKC): A downstream effector of Signal 1. Deficiency would impair TCR signaling, which is stated as normal.
    - (C) CD28: The costimulatory receptor (Signal 2). Deficiency would explain the impaired activation (reduced CD25, reduced proliferation) despite intact Signal 1.
    - (D) Interleukin-2 (IL-2): While reduced IL-2 production is a consequence of impaired activation, the primary defect described (impaired proliferation and CD25 expression *despite* TCR stimulation) points more directly to the lack of costimulation or the IL-2 receptor itself. A primary IL-2 deficiency would cause severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID), which is not consistent with the patient being immunocompetent and recovering from pneumonia. Furthermore, the vignette specifically mentions reduced *expression* of CD25 (the IL-2Rα chain), suggesting a problem *before* or *during* the IL-2 response pathway, rather than a lack of IL-2 itself. The lack of costimulation (CD28) is a common cause of reduced IL-2 production and subsequent reduced CD25 expression and proliferation.
    - (E) ZAP-70: A tyrosine kinase involved in Signal 1 (TCR signaling). Deficiency would impair TCR signaling, which is stated as normal.

##### 3. Kaplan Preclinical Immunology and Microbiology Review 2023 & First Aid 2025 Correlation
- Kaplan (pp. 62-74) details the two-signal model of T cell activation, emphasizing the necessity of both TCR engagement (Signal 1) and costimulation (Signal 2, CD28-B7) for full activation, IL-2 production, and proliferation. It highlights CD28 as the key costimulatory molecule.
- First Aid 2025 (Immunology: Lymphocyte Activation & Costimulatory Pathways) also clearly outlines the two-signal model and the roles of CD28 and B7 in providing the essential costimulatory signal for T cell activation, proliferation, and cytokine production.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) CD3ζ chain: Incorrect. The CD3ζ chain is part of the TCR complex and is essential for transmitting Signal 1. A defect here would prevent TCR signaling altogether. The vignette states that CD3 signaling molecules are normal, and the cells respond to anti-CD3, indicating Signal 1 is intact.
- (B) Protein Kinase C (PKC): Incorrect. PKC is a downstream kinase activated by Signal 1. A defect in PKC would impair the signaling cascade initiated by TCR engagement. The vignette states that CD3 signaling molecules are normal, implying PKC function is also intact for the initial signal transduction.
- (D) Interleukin-2 (IL-2): Incorrect. While IL-2 is crucial for T cell proliferation and is produced *after* activation, the primary defect described is the *failure* to activate properly (reduced CD25, reduced proliferation) *despite* TCR stimulation. A primary deficiency in IL-2 or its receptor would lead to a more severe immunodeficiency phenotype than suggested by the patient's history (immunocompetent, recovered from pneumonia). The reduced CD25 expression points to a problem earlier in the activation cascade, likely preventing sufficient IL-2 production or response. CD28 deficiency is a common cause of this.
- (E) ZAP-70: Incorrect. ZAP-70 is a tyrosine kinase associated with the TCR complex that plays a critical role in transmitting Signal 1. A defect in ZAP-70 would impair TCR signaling. The vignette states that CD3 signaling molecules are normal, implying ZAP-70 function is also intact.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | CD28 Deficiency                                  | CD3ζ Deficiency                               | PKC Deficiency                               | IL-2 Deficiency                             | ZAP-70 Deficiency                             |
| :------------------ | :---------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :-------------------------------------------- |
| **Signal 1 (TCR)**  | Intact                                          | Defective                                      | Defective                                     | Intact                                      | Defective                                     |
| **Signal 2 (Costim)** | Defective                                      | Intact                                         | Intact                                        | Intact                                      | Intact                                        |
| **CD25 Expression** | Reduced                                         | Normal (if Signal 1 occurs)                    | Normal (if Signal 1 occurs)                   | Normal                                      | Normal (if Signal 1 occurs)                   |
| **Proliferation**   | Reduced                                         | Reduced/Absent                                 | Reduced/Absent                                | Severely Reduced/Absent                     | Reduced/Absent                                |
| **IL-2 Production** | Reduced                                         | Normal (if Signal 1 occurs)                    | Normal (if Signal 1 occurs)                   | Absent/Severely Reduced                     | Normal (if Signal 1 occurs)                   |
| **Clinical Phenotype** | Variable; often mild immunodeficiency, impaired responses to certain antigens | Severe immunodeficiency (SCID-like)            | Severe immunodeficiency (SCID-like)           | Severe immunodeficiency (SCID-like)          | Severe immunodeficiency (SCID-like)            |
| **Vignette Match**  | **Best Match:** Intact Signal 1, Defective Signal 2 consequences (CD25, proliferation) | No Match: Signal 1 defective                  | No Match: Signal 1 defective                 | No Match: Primary IL-2 defect is more severe | No Match: Signal 1 defective                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The vignette demonstrates the critical role of costimulation (Signal 2, mediated by CD28-B7 interaction) in T cell activation, proliferation, and cytokine production (IL-2). Defects in Signal 1 components (TCR complex, CD3ζ, ZAP-70, PKC) or Signal 2 components (CD28, B7) lead to impaired T cell responses. While IL-2 is essential for proliferation, the primary defect described here, with reduced CD25 expression and proliferation despite intact TCR signaling, points most strongly to a deficiency in the costimulatory molecule CD28. Key differentials include defects in other components of the TCR signaling pathway (CD3ζ, ZAP-70, PKC) or primary IL-2/IL-2R deficiencies, which typically present with more severe immunodeficiency.
